The fix is to key the escape model's names per-scope rather than per-body, which is the general repair R304's guards only work around — see also R189/R195, where a name is modelled as holding one value for the whole body. Deliberately not attempted alongside the R303/R304 guards: it changes the meaning of every name in the model, and this row exists so it is not conflated with the fix that exposed it. |
| R306 umbrella: R242's corpus move states a CAUSE that points at a directory which never held the corpus, and four sibling corpora in other repos still default under `$TMPDIR` | 2026-09-07 | **OPEN — the mechanical fix is sound and verified; the recorded REASONING is wrong and the audit boundary was drawn around its own trigger** | **INSTRUMENT defect.** `bin/corpus.sh`'s move to `$HOME/.candor/corpus` works — `CORPUS_HOME` still overrides, `--hollow-check` still does not create the directory, and the new tree is verified non-hollow. But the commit blames `$TMPDIR` and the macOS `dirhelper` sweep, and **`$TMPDIR/candor-corpus` never existed**: both documented R242 victims were under `/private/tmp`, i.e. the `:-/tmp` FALLBACK branch, which `dirhelper`'s domain (the per-user `/var/folders` tree) does not explain. The claim *"migration cost is zero: `$TMPDIR/candor-corpus` does not currently exist"* checked the branch that was never used and reads as proof nothing was orphaned. Six trees under `scratchpad/swift-escape/corpus` are hollow RIGHT NOW (Alamofire 0 `.swift`, Kingfisher 0, GRDB 1, swift-collections/crypto/nio 0-1, all with `.git` stripped to `hooks/info/`) — R242 named four. **Still under `${TMPDIR}` and still treating a surviving `.git/hooks` as acquisition:** `candor-java/eval/corpus-confirmatory/run_corpus.sh:26`, `…-freshdraw/run_corpus.sh:26` (the paper's fresh-draw evidence, same path NAME), `candor-rust/soundness/confirmatory/run_frozen.sh:38`, `candor-swift/soundness/confirmatory/run_frozen.sh:24`, plus `corpus-crossorg`, `transitive-reconcile`, `rq4a-petclinic` and `candor/bin/monotone.sh:35`. And **no test pins the new default**, so a silent revert to `${TMPDIR:-/tmp}` goes unnoticed; `assert-audit.sh candor 40f713d~1..40f713d` FAILS on the commit. | Not fixed. Correct the recorded cause to the observed one (`/private/tmp`, cause not established) rather than the inferred one; port the `$HOME` default and a CONTENT-based acquisition test to the four sibling harnesses; add a test pinning `bin/corpus.sh`'s default. Relates to the standing rule that an audit's boundary must be stated, justified, and not drawn around its own trigger. |
| R307 umbrella: `corpus-ab.py --allow-unjudged` reports that it did NOT compare entries it DOES compare, and counts them in `entries_compared` | 2026-09-07 | **OPEN — behaviour is the SAFE one; both the run output and `--help` describe the opposite of what the code does** | **INSTRUMENT / EVIDENCE defect.** With `--allow-unjudged`, `excluded = bad + []`, so unjudged entries stay in `good` and their rows join the diff. Executed on a two-entry fixture (entB judged nothing in the PRE arm, 1 `Net` row in POST): the run prints *"1 entry judged NOTHING and was NOT compared"* and still reports `ADDED 1`. So a 0 → N transition DOES surface, which is the direction you want — but the text asserts the opposite, and `--help` says "They are excluded from the diff". A reader of today's R209(b) headline would compute the corpus as 1,423 crates; the diff actually spans 1,514, of which 1,276 bore any row. | Not fixed. Correct both strings to say the entries ARE compared and that an entry judging nothing in one arm and rows in the other will surface as ADDED/REMOVED. Separately: `reach_entries` stores only a COUNT, not the set, so a reach measurement cannot be cross-checked against the skipped population — store the set. |
| R308 rust: a bare pre-2018 `Fn(..)` trait object makes the whole FILE unparseable, dropping every function in it | 2026-09-07 | **OPEN — DISCLOSED via `unanalyzed`, so fail-closed rather than silent; a completeness gap, not a cardinal sin** | **COMPLETENESS, rust.** `serial-core-0.4.0` — 802 lines, 35 bodied fns, the whole crate — reports `unanalyzed: [{"path":"src/lib.rs","reason":"source failed to read/parse"}]` and **zero rows**. Bisected to a Rust-2015 bare trait object: `fn reconfigure(&mut self, setup: &Fn(&mut SerialPortSettings) -> ::Result<()>)`. Two-line repro, executed: `&Fn(&mut u8) -> ::std::io::Result<()>` beside `pub fn touches_fs(){ std::fs::read("x"); }` gives analyzed=0 / rows=0; the same file with `&dyn Fn(...)` gives analyzed=1 / rows=1. The `-> ` arrow is not required — `&Fn(&mut u8)` alone does it. **Scale, measured over all 1,563 registry crates:** 14 carry any `unanalyzed` file, 81 files total, only 2 wholly unreadable — so disclosure is working and the incidence is small. The largest, `lapin-4.10.0` with 67, is `templates/*.rs` Handlebars templates, not Rust. | Not fixed. Direction is a DISCLOSED loss, the acceptable one, but a whole crate reporting nothing is worth closing: `syn` parses `&dyn Fn(..)` and rejects the 2015 spelling, so either pre-normalise the elided `dyn` or record the parse failure per-ITEM rather than per-file. The construct is a closure trait object — the same shape as R209(b) — so ask whether java/ts/swift have an equivalent whole-file give-up. |
| R309 java: the published ADVISORY's affected list was bounded by three tables and missed a fourth, `Rules.STREAM_CONSUMING_UTILITIES` — eight commons-io and Guava methods silent on the published jars | 2026-09-07 | **CORRECTED in all three published places (umbrella CHANGELOG `ff2b9cf` + both java release bodies). Found by a review agent auditing what we published; the original text was mine** | **PUBLISHED UNDER-STATEMENT of a cardinal sin.** The advisory named 16 methods across the hand-off, stored-stream and dispatch tables and omitted `Rules.STREAM_CONSUMING_UTILITIES` (`Rules.java:613`, consumed at `Candor.java:4734` via `callArg`). Measured on jars downloaded from the releases, opaque `InputStream`/`Reader` field, ABSENT on 0.34.0 AND 0.35.0 and `Unknown` on 0.35.1: `IOUtils.skip`, `IOUtils.skipFully`, `IOUtils.copyLarge`, `IOUtils.toByteArray(in,long)` in both stream and reader spellings, plus `ByteStreams.skipFully`. Category-1 controls (`IOUtils.copy`, `toByteArray(in)`, `ByteStreams.copy`, `exhaust`) clean on all three jars. **`callArg`'s own javadoc in the fix said it "was absent from R248's own list of affected helpers"** — the boundary was known in the code and inherited by the published text. `IOUtils.copyLarge` and `skipFully` are far more common in real Java than `InputStream.skipNBytes`, which the list DID name, so a commons-io user read the advisory and concluded they were unaffected. | Corrected, not merely noted: the fourth table is now named in the advisory with its eight methods, and the correction says plainly that the earlier text told commons-io users the wrong thing. **Second lesson recorded with it:** "all four policy forms exit 0" was true of the BROKEN engine and set a false re-verification expectation — on 0.35.1 `deny <E>` and `pure` still exit 0 by design (the task is opaque; the fix changes a DISCLOSURE, not a verdict), so a user gating that way sees nothing move and concludes they were never affected. The advisory now directs them to `deny Unknown` / `candor unverified` and the `purity NOT verified` note. |
| R310 umbrella: a SCOPED one-engine cut moved `adopt/` and the IDE pins but not `ENGINE_PIN_JAVA`, so the front door kept installing the DEFECTIVE engine an advisory told users to leave | 2026-09-07 | **CLOSED for source and dev installs — candor `79a2005`. Homebrew still serves the old engine until the umbrella is re-cut, which is a publish decision** | **DELIVERY defect.** After cutting candor-java 0.35.1 to close an advisory, `bin/candor:40` was left `ENGINE_PIN_JAVA=""`, which resolves to `ENGINE_PIN` = 0.35.0. So `candor update` fetched v0.35.0, first-use autofetch fetched v0.35.0, and the CI workflow `candor init` generates curled `download/v0.35.0/candor-linux-x64` — **the exact failure `bin/candor:1618`'s own comment describes verbatim**: "otherwise a repo gated by the engine a one-engine patch fixed would have its CI install the unfixed one." The guard that would have caught it, `rs_pin_violations`, only fires when the umbrella is IN the cut, and `--only candor-java` correctly excluded it — so the tooling was right and the delivery was half-done. | **FIXED** for source/dev: `ENGINE_PIN_JAVA="0.35.1"`, verified to resolve v0.35.1 with all three artifacts returning 200 while ts/rust/swift stay on the 0.35.0 family line. **The TESTS encoded the assumption the seam exists to break** — five rows asserted the JVM route uses the FAMILY pin, true only while no engine had ever been pinned separately, so the first real one-engine patch turned them red for being right; they now resolve `ENGINE_PIN_JAVA` as `bin/candor` does, and the two "nothing diverges" controls CONSTRUCT an undiverged state instead of depending on what ships. **Residual:** Homebrew users take the umbrella tarball and keep 0.35.0 until the umbrella is re-cut; the advisory now tells them to check `candor --version` and take the artifact directly. **Standing remedy:** a scoped cut of engine E must set `ENGINE_PIN_E` in the same wave, and preflight cannot see it because the umbrella is out of scope by construction. |
| R311 rust: the coverage-gate fixture pins calibrated crates with `"*"`, so a MAJOR upstream release silently moves the ledger's REVIEWED-PURITY claim onto a version nobody reviewed | 2026-09-07 | **OPEN — the CAUSE beneath R302, found while triaging it. Measured, not inferred: the fixture, the lockfile and the rules' own cited versions disagree** | **SILENT SCOPE CREEP of a purity claim.** `generate.py --print-fixture-toml` emits `sea_orm = { package = "sea-orm", version = "*" }` — **16 of 74 deps carry a wildcard** — and `cargo fetch` resolved **sea-orm 2.0.2**, while `candor-classify`'s sea_orm rule block states in terms that it was *"verified against sea-orm 1.1.20 source"*. The same gap in the minor direction: rules cite redis 1.6.0 / mongodb 3.8.1, the fixture resolved 1.7.0 / 3.9.0. Because the ledger treats an unmatched path in a CALIBRATED crate as a claim of reviewed purity with NO disclosure (`generate.py`'s own docstring), candor asserts review of a MAJOR version it has never seen. That is what R302's 43 uncovered `sea_orm` entries are: 2.0's new types — `DatabaseExecutor` (`src/database/executor.rs:19`), `RestrictedConnection`, `RestrictedTransaction`, `RbacContext`, `RusqliteSharedConnection` — with verbs (`begin_with_options`, `transaction_async`, `transaction_with_config_async`) that appear **ZERO times** in `candor-classify/src/lib.rs`. **The generator already knows this hazard and only half-guards it:** `version_key`'s comment warns that picking the wrong registry directory means "the manifests then record entry points from a version nobody asked for, and no output says which version was read" — it fixed WHICH directory is chosen, not that the choice is unpinned and unreported. | Not fixed. Two separable remedies, and the first is cheap: **(a) the manifests and the gate output must RECORD the resolved version per crate**, so a major bump is visible in the diff instead of appearing as anonymous "newly-uncovered" rows — this alone would have made R302 self-diagnosing. **(b) Decide whether calibration is version-pinned or version-tracking.** Pinning makes the purity claim honest but freezes the corpus; tracking keeps it current but means every upstream major release silently widens a claim nobody re-reviewed. Pinning with an explicit bump step is the direction that matches the ledger's own semantics. Until then, treat the reviewed-purity claim on any wildcard-pinned calibrated crate as scoped to the version its rules cite, not to whatever resolved. |
| R315 rust: three calibrated crates CITE a reviewed version in their own rule comments and have no ceiling enforcing it — and my sweep for such citations missed all three | 2026-09-07 | **OPEN — enforcement gap, not yet a live under-report: all three sets of rules were re-verified against the newest resolved source and HOLD. Found by a baseline agent, including the defect in my own measurement** | **R311 CLASS, three more instances.** `git2` (`lib.rs:485`, *"verified against git2 0.20.4"*, resolved 0.21.0), `mysql_async` (`lib.rs:1613`, *"verified against mysql_async 0.37.0"*, resolved 0.37.1) and `tokio_postgres` (cites 0.7.10, resolved 0.7.18) each state a reviewed version and each was absent from `CALIBRATED_CEILINGS`, so the exemption was unconditional however far the source drifted. `mysql_async`'s omission looks like an oversight rather than a policy choice: its sibling `mysql` IS ceilinged. **The reason they were missed is a defect in MY sweep and is the more useful half of this row:** I searched for the crate name in DASHED form only and LINE BY LINE, so `mysql_async 0.37.0` (written with underscores) was invisible, and git2's citation — wrapped across two comment lines — was invisible too. Re-run joining wrapped comment lines and trying both name forms: **27 crates cite a version, not the 24 I reported.** A grep that under-counts reads exactly like a clean result. | Not fixed here; the three ceilings land with the baseline batch. Standing lesson: when sweeping source COMMENTS for a fact, normalise the identifier form and join wrapped lines FIRST — a comment is prose, and prose wraps. |
| R318 rust: the argon2/scrypt/pbkdf2/password_hash entropy rule is BROKEN, not stale — its only match target was deleted upstream, and the documented one-argument `hash_password()` now reads PURE | 2026-09-07 | **OPEN — a LIVE silent under-report on password-hashing crates, measured against resolved source. Found by a baseline agent, independently verified by the coordinator** | **CARDINAL SIN, rust, and on the worst possible surface.** The shared block (`candor-classify/src/lib.rs:1947-1952`) charges `Rand` on exactly one condition: `path.contains("SaltString::generate")`. **`SaltString` has ZERO occurrences in the resolved `password-hash` 0.6.1** — the type was removed in 0.6.0 (0.5.0 has 21 occurrences; verified by grep over both trees). It was replaced by free functions `generate_salt()` / `try_generate_salt()` (`0.6.1/src/lib.rs:233,243`) and, critically, by a DEFAULT TRAIT METHOD `fn hash_password(&self, password: &[u8])` (`0.6.1/src/lib.rs:107`) whose body is `let salt = try_generate_salt()?;` — i.e. **the single most documented call shape in the whole family, `argon2.hash_password(password)`, mints OS entropy and is classified `None`.** The block's `return None` fallthrough then asserts purity for every other path in all four crates. This is the sea_orm shape on a crate family whose entire purpose is a security review. | Not fixed at filing. Direction is CHARGING, so the repair is safe: match `::generate_salt`, `::try_generate_salt`, `::hash_password` and `::hash_password_with_rng` (which draws from the caller's RNG). **`ends_with`, never `contains`** — `hash_password_with_salt` takes an explicit salt and is correctly PURE, and a `contains` match would fabricate `Rand` on it. Ceiling `password_hash` and its three siblings at 0.5.x until the rewrite lands, so the old rule's claim is scoped to the version it was true for. |
| R319 rust: bcrypt grew four more internal-salt entry points between 0.15.1 and 0.19.3 and the suffix rule names none of them | 2026-09-07 | **OPEN — silent under-report at the resolved version; the rule was exactly correct when written** | **CARDINAL SIN, rust.** The rule charges `Rand` on `path.ends_with("::hash")` or `::hash_with_result` — which covered bcrypt's whole internal-salt surface at 0.15.1. Resolved 0.19.3 adds `non_truncating_hash` (`src/lib.rs:250`), `hash_bytes` (`:262`), `non_truncating_hash_bytes` (`:271`) and `non_truncating_hash_with_result` (`:293`); each documents *"The salt is generated randomly using the OS randomness"* and the `_with_result` pair reaches `getrandom::fill` directly. **None ends with `::hash` or `::hash_with_result`**, so all four read pure. The explicit-salt family (`hash_with_salt`, `hash_with_salt_bytes`, `non_truncating_hash_with_salt*`) correctly stays pure and must remain so. | Not fixed at filing. Widen the suffix list to the four new verbs — charging direction. Note the shape for the ceiling policy: this is a MINOR-version drift (0.15 -> 0.19 in 0.x terms is major-ish, but the rule broke by ADDITION, not by rename), so a ceiling would have flagged it while a stale-path check would NOT have — the old paths still exist and still work. A ceiling catches additions; a path check only catches removals. |
| R320 rust: `rand`/`rand_core` renamed `OsRng` to `SysRng` and moved it into `getrandom`, leaving the `OsRng` clause in both rule blocks matching nothing | 2026-09-07 | **CLOSED — candor-rust `867b689`. The open question was answered by MEASUREMENT and the residual turned out to be real** | **STALE RULE, rust.** Both blocks test `path.contains("OsRng")`. At resolved `rand` 0.10.2 and `rand_core` 0.10.1 the type is `SysRng`, defined in and re-exported from `getrandom`; `OsRng` appears in `rand_core` only in a doc comment. Also dead as literal strings after the 0.9/0.10 rewrite: `gen`, `gen_range`, `gen_bool`, `gen_ratio`, `gen_iter`, `thread_rng`, `from_entropy`. The replacement verbs (`random`, `random_range`, `random_bool`, `random_ratio`, `random_iter`, `rng`, `from_os_rng`) ARE already in the rule and still match, so any direct draw is still charged. | Not fixed. **The open question the agent correctly refused to answer from `candor-classify` alone:** whether a call written `rand::rngs::SysRng` resolves, in candor-scan's own path resolution, to crate_name `rand`/`rand_core` (hitting the dead clause) or to `getrandom` (whole-crate exempt regardless). If the former, a handle passed around without ever hitting a suffix-matched verb could be missed. Settle that by reading the resolver and building the fixture, THEN decide the repair — do not delete the clause on the assumption it is redundant. **ANSWERED 2026-09-08 by the fixture the row asked for: it is the FORMER.** candor resolves the crate from the path the CONSUMER writes, and rand 0.10 re-exports getrandom's type (`rand-0.10.2/src/rngs/mod.rs:119`), so `rand::rngs::SysRng` arrives with crate_name `rand` and lands in the `rand` block — the re-export does not carry getrandom's whole-crate exemption across. **The residual is therefore real, and the agent's caution was right: it is not a precision loss, it is a lost SAFETY MARGIN.** `contains("OsRng")` is not a duplicate of the verb list; it is what charges an UNRECOGNISED verb on a type that exists only to draw from the OS. Executed: `OsRng` + an unrecognised verb -> `['Rand']`, `SysRng` + the same verb -> **ABSENT**. The margin protected the deleted spelling and not its replacement. **FIXED — candor-rust `867b689`:** the clause is mirrored for `SysRng` with the same `clone`/`fork`/`default` exemption, for the same reason (a unit struct, `getrandom-0.4.3/src/sys_rng.rs:34`, so copying the handle consumes nothing). Both spellings and both exemptions are asserted in ONE test so the pair cannot drift apart again; falsified against the pre-fix classifier, where the `SysRng` row fails and the rest pass. **REACH IS ZERO AND THAT IS RECORDED, NOT HIDDEN.** `bin/corpus-ab.py` over 9 entries / 4,807 rows (quinn-proto, crypto-common, rand, rand_core, getrandom, rustls, rauthy, atuin, vaultwarden): ADDED 0 / REMOVED 0 / CHANGED 0 with the marked branch counting **0 hits**. This is a margin for a spelling today's corpus does not yet contain, rand 0.10 being new — **not** a clause that cannot fire, which is the distinction from the dead clauses this register criticised on 2026-09-08 (R331's `TlsConnectorBuilder::build` could never be reached at all; this one is demonstrated firing on a legal, compiling spelling). **And the fixture built to settle this row found a LARGER hole one argument along — see R334.** R320's framing ("a rename residual") would have sent the audit past it, because the hole is present in the OLD spelling too. |
| R321 rust: `terminal_colorsaurus` 1.0.3 added a PURE accessor with the same trailing segment as its effectful free function, and the suffix rule charges `Ipc` to both | 2026-09-07 | **OPEN — OVER-report, the safe direction, introduced by exactly the version drift this exercise is about** | **FABRICATION, rust.** The rule matches `path.ends_with("::theme_mode")` and charges `Ipc`. At 0.4.8 that was unambiguous. Resolved 1.0.3 has TWO: the free `pub fn theme_mode(options)` (`src/lib.rs:157`), which really does terminal I/O via `color_palette(options)`, and `pub fn ColorPalette::theme_mode(&self)` (`src/lib.rs:112`), a pure derived accessor comparing `perceived_lightness()` on two ALREADY-FETCHED colours. Both end in `::theme_mode`; both are charged. | Not fixed. Replace the suffix with an FQN-exact match on the free function (`path == "terminal_colorsaurus::theme_mode"`), leaving the accessor unmatched. **Worth keeping as the counter-example to R319:** the same mechanism — an upstream release ADDING a name — produces a silent under-report in one crate and a fabrication in another, depending only on whether the new name is effectful. A suffix rule is a bet that no future release will reuse the leaf name, and that bet is being lost in both directions. |
| R312 rust: the `native_tls_crate` rule is DEAD IN PRODUCTION — `classify()` is asked the RESOLVED package name while the coverage gate feeds it the CALIBRATED_CRATES identifier, and the gate records the dead rule as COVERED | 2026-09-07 | **CLOSED — candor-rust `bbd7615` (gate widened in the same commit that found it). Status corrected 2026-09-08: this column said OPEN while the remedy column said FIXED, which is how a closed row gets re-filed by the next agent that greps status** | **CARDINAL SIN + INSTRUMENT, rust.** `classify()` receives the resolved package name, `native_tls` (cargo normalises `native-tls`). The rule at `lib.rs:956` is gated `crate_name == "native_tls_crate"` — the CALIBRATED_CRATES *identifier* — so `TlsConnector::new` and `Identity::from_pkcs8` can never match in a real scan, while `eval/coverage-gate/covered.tsv:972-973` records both as COVERED. **Two paths computing one fact, disagreeing in the reassuring direction (brief §G).** Executed: `probe_nativetls_identity_from_pkcs8` and `probe_nativetls_tlsconnector_new` are ABSENT from the report, while the sibling `control_nativetls_connect` charges `Net` through the separate `"native_tls"` arm — so the file parsed and the instrument can fire. **Second half, worse:** under a `native_tls_crate = { package = "native-tls" }` alias the ledger key is the syntactic first segment, which IS in CALIBRATED_CRATES — so the call is silently EXEMPT while no rule can run. Under a plain `native-tls` dependency the same calls correctly disclose `invisible: ["native_tls"]`. Boundary widened past the trigger: `generate.py`'s `RENAME` map has 16 entries and this is the ONLY one where the identifier is not `package.replace('-','_')`. | **FIXED** in the same commit by widening the gate to `matches!(crate_name, "native_tls" \| "native_tls_crate")`. Corpus A/B for that widening ALONE: ADDED 5, CHANGED 133 — redis ×52, ureq ×48, hyper-tls ×6, tcp-stream ×5, tungstenite ×4, sqlx-core ×3 — all genuine propagation from `TlsConnector::new()`, ground-truthed on three of them. **Residual NOT closed:** a consumer who invents their own `package = ` alias for any of the 82 calibrated crates reproduces the same split. Only the RENAME map was swept; arbitrary consumer aliases were not. |
| R313 rust: the `postgres` ceiling I recorded was TOKIO-postgres's version, lifted by my own regex from a comment about a different crate | 2026-09-07 | **CLOSED by REMOVAL — candor-rust `bbd7615`. My defect, found by a rules agent** | **PROVENANCE defect in the R311 machinery itself.** I recorded `("postgres", "0.7.10")`. `postgres` ships **0.19.14**; `0.7.10` appears in the shared PG rule block as a citation about **tokio-postgres** (`query_typed` is tokio_postgres 0.7.10+). My sweep matched a version-shaped string near the crate name without checking which crate the sentence was ABOUT. Consequence: `postgres` permanently exceeded its ceiling — the safe direction, but on a false premise — while `tokio_postgres`, the crate the citation actually concerned, had no ceiling at all. The mirror case is `sqlx_core`, whose ceiling is 0.8.6 while its own comment says "verified against sqlx-core 0.8.6/0.9.0". | **FIXED.** `postgres` is now deliberately ABSENT from `CALIBRATED_CEILINGS`, with the reason stated in the code: no verified baseline exists, and inventing one would be exactly the claim-without-review the table exists to stop. `tokio_postgres` carries the 0.7.18 a baseline agent verified. **The general lesson is the one worth keeping: a version string NEAR a crate name is not a version string ABOUT it** — the same class as R315's dashed-form/wrapped-line misses, and both were mine, and both read as clean results. |
| R322 rust: the calibration ceiling looked up TWO KEY SPACES and crossed neither — R312's shape reintroduced inside R311's own fix, the same day | 2026-09-07 | **CLOSED — candor-rust `696db56`. MINE. Found by a plan review run BEFORE the work rather than after** | **SILENT-DIRECTION defect in the containment mechanism itself.** The exemption is keyed on `cr` (the syntactic first segment a consumer writes, `scan.rs`); the ceiling lookup was keyed on `real` (the resolved package name after `dep_renames`). `CALIBRATED_CEILINGS` is populated from CALIBRATED_CRATES IDENTIFIERS, so `("native_tls_crate", "0.2.18")` sat under a key `real` can never hold — the ceiling could never fire while `cr` still took the exemption. **The first repair was ALSO wrong and an executed fixture caught it:** checking each key against itself finds a version with no ceiling and a ceiling with no version, and concludes "not beyond review". Under a `package =` alias the lock records `native_tls` while the ceiling is under `native_tls_crate` — the spaces must be CROSSED. Executed both arms: a consumer aliasing native-tls at 0.9.9 against a 0.2.18 ceiling was ABSENT (silently exempt) and now reports `invisible: ["native_tls_crate"]`. **Second defect, same commit:** `registry_lock_versions` read only `{dir}/Cargo.lock`, so a WORKSPACE MEMBER got no version and kept the exemption; `find_workspace_root` already existed for exactly this and was not consulted, and the comment calling the lockless case an accepted residual is what stopped it being measured. | **FIXED.** Version looked up under either key, ceiling tested under both, either one exceeding is enough — the disagreement between two key spaces must never be what grants silence. Workspace root consulted when the member has no lock of its own. 29/29 gates. **Standing lesson: when a fact is keyed twice, the bug is not in either lookup — it is that nobody crossed them.** |
| R323 rust: R304's `one_entity` guard falls back to SUPPRESSION, so an ordinary shadowed or sibling-scope closure name stays silent | 2026-09-07 | **OPEN — PRE-EXISTING, not a regression: measured ABSENT on the pre-fix binary too. Filed because the guard's FALLBACK DIRECTION was never stated, and it is the cardinal-sin direction** | **SILENT UNDER-REPORT, rust, residual of R303/R304.** The R304 guard requires `name_bindings[name] == 1`; when that fails the body is pushed to `escapes`, i.e. SUPPRESSED. So `let c = \|\| H::new("a"); c(); let c = \|\| H::new("b"); c();` — two bindings of one name, both invoked and discarded, **both dropping in frame** — reports ABSENT, as does the sibling-block spelling `{ let c = ..; c(); } { let c = ..; c(); }`. Executed on both binaries: ABSENT before the fix and ABSENT after; the single-binding control correctly moved ABSENT → `['Fs']`. **The point of the row is the framing error, not the cells:** my own review brief asked which BINDING FORMS the guard might miss — the fabrication direction — when the question that mattered was *in which direction does `one_entity == false` fail?* It fails silent, on legal everyday code. | Not fixed. Same root as R305: the counters key on a bare identifier with no scope, so two bindings of one name are indistinguishable from one name used twice. The real repair is per-SCOPE keying in the escape model, which is the same model change R189/R195/R297 need — do not patch it locally. Until then the residual is stated rather than implied, which is the whole reason this row exists. |
| R333 rust: the FALLIBLE draw verbs `try_next_u32`/`try_next_u64` are unmatched — the natural API on rand 0.10's `SysRng` reads PURE | 2026-09-07 | **CLOSED — candor-rust `e414faf`. Status corrected 2026-09-08 (the remedy column already said FIXED)** | **CARDINAL SIN, rust.** `rand_core` 0.10.1 defines two traits: `RngCore` (`next_u32`/`next_u64`/`fill_bytes`) and **`TryRngCore` (`try_next_u32`/`try_next_u64`/`try_fill_bytes`)**. The rule matches only the first three by suffix, and `ends_with("::next_u32")` does NOT match `::try_next_u32`. Since `SysRng` is a FALLIBLE RNG, the `try_*` forms are its natural API. Executed on the current binary: `r.try_next_u32()` and `r.try_next_u64()` are **ABSENT**, while the `next_u32`/`fill_bytes` controls are `['Rand']`. (`try_fill_bytes` IS charged, by a different route — worth understanding before patching, because whatever catches it may be the better general fix.) | Not fixed at filing — candor-rust was held by review agents. Charging direction: add the three `try_*` suffixes. **R320's framing was wrong and this row supersedes that half of it:** R320 concluded the dead `OsRng` clause cost nothing because "the verb matches still catch a direct draw". They catch the INFALLIBLE ones. The rename from `OsRng` to `SysRng` came with a move to a fallible trait, and the rule tracked neither. **The general shape, third instance today after R319 and R321: an upstream rename is rarely just a rename — it arrives with an API shape change, and a suffix list written against the old shape silently stops covering the new one.** **FIXED — candor-rust `e414faf`.** Both `try_next_u32` and `try_next_u64` now charge `Rand`, with the infallible controls unchanged. **The fix took two attempts and the first one is the lesson: TWO VERB LISTS answer the one question "is this a draw?" — one in the `rand_core` block and one in `rand`'s — and widening only the first moved nothing.** Correct code on a path the call does not take, which is byte-identical from the outside to a fix that does not work. That is the same failure as tonight's R197 attempt, twice in one evening, and both were caught only by re-measuring rather than by assuming the edit had landed. |
| R330 rust: my own R318 fix FABRICATES on password-hash 0.5.x — `ends_with("::hash_password")` charges `Rand` on the EXPLICIT-SALT, deterministic call | 2026-09-08 | **CLOSED — candor-rust `3a96651`. Fixed by ARITY, not by version** | **FABRICATION, rust, self-inflicted, and version-bound.** R318's premise holds only at password-hash **0.6.x**, where `hash_password(&self, password)` is the one-argument default whose body is `try_generate_salt()?`. At **0.5.0** (`traits.rs:33`) and argon2 **0.5.3** (`lib.rs:572`) the same NAME is `hash_password(&self, password, salt)` — the caller supplies the salt and the call is deterministic recomputation. The `ends_with`-over-`contains` guard I relied on is keyed on the 0.6.x name, and at 0.5.x the explicit-salt call IS named `hash_password`. Executed, compiling fixture: two calls with the same stored salt produce byte-identical hashes (`EQ=true`) while `SaltString::generate` differs; `rehash_with_stored_salt` (argon2 0.5.3), `scrypt_explicit_bound`, `pbkdf2_explicit_bound` and the UFCS spelling all went pure → `["Rand"]`. The 0.6.1 half is correct and separately verified. | Not fixed. **The structural finding is worth more than the row: `calibration_exceeded` is ONE-SIDED — it fires only when `resolved > ceiling`, so a ceiling can never see a rule that mis-describes an OLDER version. A version ceiling catches rules that go stale FORWARD; a rule rewritten FOR a new API silently mis-describes the old one, and nothing in the R311 design looks in that direction.** Four of the 70 ceiling rows sit on exactly this cliff. Remedy needs the classifier to see the resolved version — which R311's `lock_versions` now makes possible for the first time — or the clause must be narrowed to a spelling unique to 0.6.x. **FIXED by a THIRD route neither of those named: the call-site ARITY.** Both signatures are `hash_password`, so the path cannot separate them; but 0.5.x is `(password, salt)` and 0.6.x is `(password)`, and the argument count is written at every call site and needs no lockfile. `Call` gains `argc` (cache format rev24 -> rev25), the collector records it at the two visitors that have an argument list, and `candor_classify::password_hash_explicit_salt` adjudicates it. `syn` gives a method call its args WITHOUT the receiver and a UFCS `Trait::method(&recv, ..)` WITH it, so the count is normalized against the `method` flag the `Call` already carried — without that, one of the two spellings keeps fabricating. **The version route was considered and rejected for the reason this row itself found:** `calibration_exceeded` is one-sided, so a ceiling can never see a rule that mis-describes an OLDER release, which is precisely this direction; and it would answer nothing where no lockfile resolves. **Direction, stated so it cannot drift:** `classify` still charges `::hash_password` unconditionally and the arity only NARROWS that charge. An unrecorded arity (`argc == 0` — every synthesized edge, and every cache entry written before the field existed) leaves the charge standing, so the refinement can remove a fabrication but can never create a silence. The `argc == 0` sentinel is only sound because neither signature is reachable with an empty argument list under either spelling; a test asserts that direction rather than assuming it. **A/B, and the value figure is smaller than the defect and worth recording as such.** Falsified against a pre-fix binary built from `e414faf` in a worktree: the two 0.5.x spellings move `['Rand']` -> ABSENT while the three that must charge (`v06_method`, `v06_ufcs`, `with_rng`) are unchanged. On a real-crate corpus (`bin/corpus-ab.py`, three cloned argon2 consumers — rauthy, atuin, vaultwarden — 3,856 rows) the branch REACHED real code **6 times across 2 entries** and the row diff is **ADDED 0 / REMOVED 0 / CHANGED 0**. That is not a null result, it is the reason the fabrication survived review: in the dominant real idiom the explicit salt is minted one line above the call (`let salt = SaltString::generate(&mut OsRng); argon2.hash_password(pw, &salt)` — rauthy `utils/hash_password.rs:63`, atuin `handlers/user.rs:263`), so a legitimate `Rand` masks the fabricated one at function granularity. **The over-charge is only visible where the salt is LOADED rather than minted — the re-hash / verify-against-stored-hash path — which is exactly the fixture the reviewer built and exactly the call a security review is reading.** Also measured: a second A/B over the eight registry crates that DEFINE these APIs reached the branch zero times, because an intra-crate call is not a dependency call; recorded because a zero-reach A/B is safety-only and says nothing (`corpus-ab.py` refused it, correctly). **THE CLASS WAS SWEPT, not just the instance, and the boundary is stated because the sweep is imperfect.** The class is: a rule keyed on a NAME where the same name carries a DIFFERENT SIGNATURE in another release — Rust has no overloading, so the hazard is entirely cross-version, and it points BOTH ways (a rule written for the new shape fabricates on the old; one written for the old shape misses the new). Swept every `ends_with("::NAME")` and exact-path target in `candor-classify` against every calibrated crate with more than one version in the local registry cache: **50 rule-target names moved arity, of which the sweep's own known positive — `password_hash::hash_password` `{0.5.0: [2], 0.6.1: [1]}` — came back, which is the calibration that makes the rest readable.** Every other hit resolved to one of three non-defects, checked individually: a name belonging to a DIFFERENT crate's rule (the sweep is crate-blind — `::call` fired on `mysql_async` but the rule is scoped `crate_name == "ureq"`), a union across unrelated same-named methods in one crate, or a `#[cfg(test)]` helper (`rand::test::rng(seed)`, `rand-0.10.2/src/lib.rs:324`, which is not public API). **No second instance.** What the sweep CANNOT see, stated rather than implied: calibrated crates with only one version on disk, arity changes outside the versions present, and — the real limitation — it flags arity movement, when the defect requires the movement to change whether an ARGUMENT supplies the resource. That is why the entropy tier is where the class bites and the `Net`/`Db` tiers are not: `ureq::get(url)` is `Net` at any arity. |
| R331 rust: `TlsConnectorBuilder::build` is where native-tls's `Fs` actually happens, and no rule names it | 2026-09-08 | **OPEN — silent under-report adjacent to the R312 clause widened the same day; found by widening the audit boundary past its own trigger** | **CARDINAL SIN, rust.** `native_tls::TlsConnector::new()` is literally `TlsConnector::builder().build()` (`native-tls-0.2.18/src/lib.rs:481`), and `TlsConnectorBuilder::build` (`:451`) is where `imp::TlsConnector::new` — the openssl-backend cert-file/dir load — actually runs. Executed: `only_connector_new` → `["Fs"]`, `only_builder_build` → **pure**. The builder spelling is the DOMINANT real-world idiom whenever any option is set: `redis-1.6.0/src/aio/tokio.rs:128,134`, `sqlx-core-0.8.6/src/net/tls/tls_native_tls.rs:45`, `tcp-stream-0.34.14/src/native_tls_impl.rs:39`, `ureq-3.4.1/src/tls/native_tls.rs:146` — in all four the enclosing fn is only INCIDENTALLY covered by a neighbouring `TlsConnector::new` or `Identity::from_pkcs8`, so a fresh consumer doing only `builder().danger_accept_invalid_certs(true).build()` reads pure. Sibling checked before claiming a class: `TlsAcceptor::new`/`builder().build()` are NOT affected — `imp::TlsAcceptor::new` on openssl touches no filesystem, so their absence is correct. | Not fixed. Charging direction: add `TlsConnectorBuilder::build` to the same clause. Note it is a CROSS-PLATFORM union like its neighbours — `Fs` on the openssl backend, pure on security-framework and schannel — and the reviewer's wider point should be recorded with it: **no single target platform makes all three native-tls clauses true at once**, which the clause's comment does not say. **FIX ATTEMPTED AND REVERTED 2026-09-08 — the blocker is one level below the rule.** Adding `ends_with("TlsConnectorBuilder::build")` to the clause changed NOTHING, and probing rather than theorising shows why: `native_tls::TlsConnector::builder().build()` and its two-statement sibling `let b = TlsConnector::builder(); b.build();` are **both absent from `functions[]` entirely, with no `calls` recorded at all** — while `TlsConnector::new()` in the same file charges `Fs`. The scanner never types `TlsConnector::builder()` as a builder, so `.build()` resolves to no path and NO suffix can match it. The rule was correct and unreachable. Reverted rather than left in place, because a clause that can never fire is exactly the dead weight this register has criticised three times today (R320's `OsRng`, `etcetera::data_local_dir`, `jiff::Zoned::now_with`). | Not fixed. **The real work is receiver typing for this builder chain, not a classifier suffix** — `ctor_type`'s builder-chain walk is guarded by a type-CHANGE blocklist, and something in that path declines `TlsConnector::builder`. Start by instrumenting which guard rejects it. **Third instance tonight of one failure mode — R197, R333's first half, and this — where correct code sat on a path the failing case never takes.** In all three the edit looked right, the suite stayed green, and only re-measuring the fixture showed the fix had not landed. A green suite after a fix is not evidence the fix fired. |
| R332 umbrella: `assert-audit.sh` was blind to this project's OWN safety vocabulary, on the commit where it mattered | 2026-09-08 | **CLOSED — candor `1e0d591`** | **INSTRUMENT defect.** `bash bin/assert-audit.sh candor-rust bbd7615^..bbd7615` reported *"no safety assertions added — nothing to defend"*. That diff asserts, in comments, *"is deterministic recomputation — correctly PURE"* and *"The explicit-salt family … stays PURE"* — and **one of them was FALSE** (R330, found by a different agent an hour later). `ASSERT_RE` carried proven/guaranteed/inert/by-construction and had no term for `pure`, the word this codebase actually uses when it means "I checked, it is safe". The commit added 117 lines and zero test assertions, which is precisely the `7ecda11` shape the tool exists to fail on. | **FIXED** by widening `ASSERT_RE` with the house dialect (`correctly pure`, `stays pure`, `remains pure`, `is pure`, `verified still`, `deterministic recomputation`). Verified both ways: it now flags both lines of `bbd7615` and fails that range, while a no-assertion commit still passes and the selftest plus release-test's §17 history fixtures stay green. **A detector blind to the local idiom returns the reassuring answer exactly where the idiom is load-bearing.** |
| R334 rust: the ENTROPY SOURCE passed as an ARGUMENT is invisible — `StdRng::try_from_rng(&mut SysRng)` reads pure, and so does the old `from_rng(&mut OsRng)` | 2026-09-08 | **OPEN — PRE-EXISTING and NOT a rename residual: measured identical in BOTH spellings, so it predates R320 and R320's framing would have sent the audit past it. Found by the fixture built to settle R320** | **CARDINAL SIN, rust.** Every entropy rule in the `rand` block keys on the CALLEE — a verb (`from_os_rng`, `next_u32`, `fill_bytes`) or the source TYPE in receiver position (`contains("OsRng")`/`contains("SysRng")`). The idiomatic way to seed a userspace CSPRNG from the OS puts the source in ARGUMENT position, where neither key can see it. Executed on the current binary, one fixture, four rows: `StdRng::try_from_rng(&mut SysRng)` **ABSENT**; `StdRng::from_rng(&mut OsRng)` **ABSENT**; `StdRng::from_seed([0u8; 32])` ABSENT (correct — deterministic, and the control that shows why `from_rng` cannot simply be charged); `StdRng::from_os_rng()` `['Rand']` (the verb form, correctly charged). **Real instances, not a constructed shape:** `quinn-proto-0.11.17/src/endpoint.rs:81` — `StdRng::try_from_rng(&mut SysRng)` seeding a QUIC endpoint's RNG; `crypto-common-0.2.2/src/generate.rs:30` — `Self::try_generate_from_rng(&mut SysRng)`; `zmij` tests. On quinn-proto the enclosing `Endpoint::new` reports `['Unknown']`, so the draw is INCIDENTALLY covered by an unrelated unresolved call rather than charged — the R331 pattern; in a body with nothing else the same call is fully ABSENT. | Not fixed, and deliberately not guessed at. **The charging direction is NOT simply to add `::from_rng`/`::try_from_rng`:** their effect depends on the argument, `from_rng(&mut pcg)` is deterministic, and 802 `from_seed`/`seed_from_u64` call sites in the local registry cache say the deterministic population is the larger one. That is R330's exact shape one crate over — a name whose effect is decided by what is passed to it — and R330's own lesson is that the discriminator must be read, not assumed. The discriminator here is the ARGUMENT'S TYPE, which the scanner does not currently record: `Call` carries `str_arg` (a string literal at position 0) and now `argc`, but nothing about what an argument RESOLVES to. Precedent for reading an argument exists (`resolve_host_arg`, `positional_str_lit`), so the shape of the repair is: record whether any argument resolves to a path naming a calibrated entropy source, and charge on that. **Before building it, price it against the alternative** — a whole-crate `Rand` on `rand` would close this and every future spelling at the cost of fabricating on the deterministic majority, and the block's own comment says that over-report is exactly what the verb list was written to remove. Do not undo that without the number. |

## ⚠ OWED TO TOM — a gate-semantics question raised by R105's fix (2026-09-02)

R105 replaced "pick the `#[cfg]` arm that happens to be last in the file" with an honest
`Unknown` + `ambiguous:` when the arms classify differently. That is unambiguously more truthful. **But it
changes what a bare `deny <E>` does, and the change deserves a ruling rather than a default.**

Measured on two crates identical but for arm order, one arm `Fs` and one `Env`:

| policy | pre-fix (arm A) | pre-fix (arm B) | post-fix (both) |
|---|---|---|---|
| `deny Fs` | 1 | 0 | **0** |
| `deny Unknown` | 0 | 0 | **1** |
| `deny Fs Unknown` | 0 | 0 | **1** |

Post-fix the two orders agree under every form — that is the point, and it is right. The question is the
first row. **Pre-fix, `deny Fs` fired for roughly half the affected crates by coin-flip. Post-fix it never
fires**, because the honest answer is `Unknown` and a bare `deny <E>` does not act on `Unknown`.

**The engine now KNOWS `Fs` is one of exactly two possibilities and does not fail closed on it.** That is
defensible — `deny Unknown` is the documented upgrade and it does fire — but it is a different posture
from the rest of the family, where an effect the engine can name is denied. The alternative is: **a
`deny <E>` fires when `<E>` appears among the arms of an `ambiguous:` disclosure.**

This is a **cross-engine SPEC question**, not a rust one — java/ts/swift have not been measured for the
same shape. No engine or spec text was changed. **Tom's call.**

## THE REUSABLE QUESTION FROM THE R109–R115 VEIN (2026-09-02)

Seven rows came out of one week-old-looking gap, and the question that produced five of them is not about
web globals at all:

> **Where does the mechanism that answers a question actually FIRE, and does the syntax that ASKS the
> question ever reach it?**

**"Not charged" and "not reachable by this mechanism" are different facts, and only the second one tells
you where to look next.** R111 wrote that distinction down as an aside — that `timeOrigin` and
`nodeTiming` are property accesses while the classify site fires only on calls — and that aside found
R113, R114 and R115.

Three corollaries, each measured rather than reasoned:

1. **A curated table is bounded by its call site, not by its contents.** Both of candor-ts's web-global
   tables are exhaustive-looking and sit under `isCallExpression || isNewExpression`. No amount of adding
   members reaches a property.
2. **Agreement between two resolution paths is worthless as a signal** (R111). R113 is the fourth instance
   of both-arms-agreeing-while-both-are-wrong, and it was sitting inside a comment that used the agreement
   as the reason to dismiss it.
3. **The same site can fail in both directions at once.** R113/R114 are silent under-reports; R115 is a
   fabrication that fails `deny Fs` on a function that performs nothing — same property machinery, opposite
   errors. Auditing only the miss direction would have found three of four.

**And the class turned out BOUNDED, which is the actionable part.** A table keyed on resolved-declaration
identity fires on **30 of 28,730 property accesses (0.104%)** across six real repos — as narrow as the
call-arm branches it would sit beside. The naive fear ("charging property reads would be catastrophic") is
true only of the shape nobody proposed: 52% of those accesses fall in the no-symbol bucket. **Treat 0.104%
as a floor** — that corpus is TS libraries, and `document.cookie` appears once in it, `localStorage.x = v`
zero times; a browser-application corpus would price it differently and was not built.
